      "content": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nReasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \\textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Before building production detectors, a prior question must be answered: can current probing methods reliably distinguish pre-CP from post-CP reasoning steps, and under what conditions? This paper provides a systematic characterization of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect.\n\nExisting safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. Before asking which modality is better, a prior methodological question must be answered: how sensitive are probe conclusions to the choice of layer selection metric? HS probes require choosing which transformer layer to monitor, a researcher degree of freedom whose metric sensitivity has received almost no scrutiny. We show this choice alone can reverse a study's central conclusion.\n\nWe present an initial characterization across four reasoning models---primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models), with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence---at two scales (7--8B, 32B) on the HarmThoughts benchmark. Our central finding is methodological: the choice of layer selection metric entirely determines whether an HS advantage is observed. A composite metric (balanced accuracy $\\times$ crossing rate) selects shallow layers for 32B models because shallow representations compress directional information, so a linear probe operates near the noise floor and threshold crossings reflect noise rather than semantic transitions. At these layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage.\n\nThreshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) share a common confusion matrix and converge by mathematical necessity; BA independently confirms deep-layer selection (\\cref{tab:layer_selection}). At these layers, the HS advantage is graded: \\emph{confirmatory} at 7--8B (precision +20.1 to +21.9pp, nested CV validated; \\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}), \\emph{significant} but position-competitive at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp; \\cref{sec:32b-analysis}), and \\emph{exploratory} at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, position-mediated, below MDE; \\cref{sec:experiments}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Layer selection sensitivity.} We identify a systematic bias in composite metrics that reverses HS probe conclusions at 32B scale. A five-metric ablation establishes principled selection criteria (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Encoder-quality boundary with evidence gradient.} Against 384d MiniLM, the HS advantage is confirmatory at 7--8B (+20.1 to +21.9pp, HB corrected, nested CV validated), significant but position-competitive at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp), and exploratory at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, position-mediated). Against 1024d BGE-large, only OT-7B survives correction (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Negative results define method boundaries.} No alternative method consistently matches supervised probing at any evidence tier (\\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD/CUSUM fail on gradual transitions; Mahalanobis distance finds near-zero separation; RepE shows inconsistent performance across models (competitive on QwQ-32B, poor elsewhere); cross-model transfer fails under PCA alignment. Control task selectivity (11.1--16.8pp above chance; \\cref{app:control}) confirms genuine safety structure.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\noindent These findings provide initial characterization on one benchmark for hidden-state safety monitoring and identify layer selection as a critical confound that future work must address.\n",
      "numLines": 21,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 21
    }
  },

```text
43	\label{app:layer_sweep}
44	
45	For each model, we sweep all transformer layers and evaluate five candidate selection metrics: (1)~balanced accuracy $\times$ crossing rate (the CR-composite), (2)~pure balanced accuracy, (3)~precision, (4)~inverse pre-CP FPR, and (5)~an additive composite. The CR-composite systematically selects shallow layers for 32B models because early-layer representations exhibit higher crossing rates (more frequent threshold crossings) regardless of discriminative power. Three threshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) converge on deep layers for all models; pure balanced accuracy selects layers whose depth varies with the data split (under 70/30: L30 for QwQ-32B, L47 for R1-32B; under the canonical 60/20/20: L63 and L46 respectively; \cref{tab:ba_layer_results}).
46	
47	\paragraph{Selected layers.} R1-8B: all five metrics agree on layer~14 (44\% depth). OT-7B: CR-composite selects L0 (0\%), threshold-based consensus selects L16 (57\%). QwQ-32B: CR-composite selects L2 (3\%), threshold-based consensus selects L63 (98\%). R1-32B: CR-composite selects L10 (16\%), threshold-based consensus selects L63 (98\%). The disagreement is most extreme for 64-layer models, where the CR-composite selects layers in the bottom 16\% of the network while threshold-based metrics select layers at 98\% depth. Under 70/30, pure balanced accuracy selects L30 (47\%) for QwQ-32B and L47 (73\%) for R1-32B; under the canonical 60/20/20, it selects L63 (98\%) and L46 (72\%), which demonstrates sensitivity to the data split.
48	
49	\paragraph{Impact on modality comparison.} At the CR-composite layers, HS vs.\ text precision $p$-values are 0.064 (R1-32B) and 0.35--0.83 (QwQ-32B), both non-significant. At the threshold-based deep layers, all $p$-values fall below 0.005. The initially reported ``scale-dependent'' pattern (HS advantage at 7--8B but not 32B) was entirely an artifact of layer selection methodology, not a genuine property of model scale (\cref{sec:layer-results}).
50	
51	\paragraph{Layer selection stability (LOO-CV).} Leave-one-out cross-validation on the validation set shows moderate consistency for 7--8B models (65--68\% dominant-layer agreement) but lower for 32B, confirming that layer selection instability is particularly acute at small sample sizes. Under pure balanced accuracy, the dominant layer is selected in 68\% (QwQ-32B: L30, 13/19 folds) and 65\% (R1-32B: L47, 13/20 folds) of folds. Alternative selections scatter across neighboring layers (L29, L55--56 for QwQ; L32, L38, L43 for R1-32B), reinforcing the need for multi-metric auditing rather than single-metric layer selection. Threshold-based metrics show tighter convergence to L63 for both 32B models.
52	
53	\paragraph{Joint (layer, $W$) sensitivity.} Under the original 70/30 validation protocol, we evaluate QwQ-32B across 64 layers $\times$ 3 windows ($W \in \{1, 3, 5\}$) (192 configurations) (\cref{tab:joint_layer_w}). L60 is near-optimal: within 2.1pp of the global best at $W{=}3$ and the top-ranked layer at $W{=}5$. (Under 60/20/20, threshold-based metrics select L63; the shift from L60 to L63 across splits itself illustrates layer selection sensitivity.) The optimal layer shifts from middle-deep (L46--47 at $W{=}1$) to deep (L60 at $W{=}5$), which confirms a layer-window interaction. L30, selected by per-fold LOO-CV at $W{=}1$ under balanced accuracy, is suboptimal across all windows (max BA $= 0.769$ vs.\ global best 0.817). Joint (layer, $W$) stability was evaluated via grid search on $W \in \{1, 3, 5\}$ for QwQ-32B only; full leave-one-fold-out validation of the (layer, $W$) pair and extension to R1-32B remain for future work.
54	
55	\begin{table}[h]
56	\centering
57	\small
58	\caption{QwQ-32B joint (layer, $W$) grid search under 70/30 validation. L60 is near-optimal at recommended windows ($W{=}3$--$5$). L30 (LOO-CV selection at $W{=}1$) is suboptimal across all windows. Under the canonical 60/20/20 split, threshold-based metrics select L63.}
59	\label{tab:joint_layer_w}
60	\begin{tabular}{clccc}
61	\toprule
62	$W$ & \textbf{Best layer} & \textbf{Best BA} & \textbf{L60 BA} & \textbf{L60 rank} \\

65	3 & L46 & 0.817 & 0.796 & $\sim$6th \\

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nReasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \\textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Before building production detectors, a prior question must be answered: can current probing methods reliably distinguish pre-CP from post-CP reasoning steps, and under what conditions? This paper provides a systematic characterization of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect.\n\nExisting safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. Before asking which modality is better, a prior methodological question must be answered: how sensitive are probe conclusions to the choice of layer selection metric? HS probes require choosing which transformer layer to monitor, a researcher degree of freedom whose metric sensitivity has received almost no scrutiny. We show this choice alone can reverse a study's central conclusion.\n\nWe present an initial characterization across four reasoning models---primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models), with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence---at two scales (7--8B, 32B) on the HarmThoughts benchmark. Our central finding is methodological: the choice of layer selection metric entirely determines whether an HS advantage is observed. A composite metric (balanced accuracy $\\times$ crossing rate) selects shallow layers for 32B models because shallow representations compress directional information, so a linear probe operates near the noise floor and threshold crossings reflect noise rather than semantic transitions. At these layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage.\n\nThreshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) share a common confusion matrix and converge by mathematical necessity; BA independently confirms deep-layer selection (\\cref{tab:layer_selection}). At these layers, the HS advantage is graded: \\emph{confirmatory} at 7--8B (precision +20.1 to +21.9pp, nested CV validated; \\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}), \\emph{significant} but position-competitive at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp; \\cref{sec:32b-analysis}), and \\emph{exploratory} at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, position-mediated, below MDE; \\cref{sec:experiments}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Layer selection sensitivity.} We identify a systematic bias in composite metrics that reverses HS probe conclusions at 32B scale. A five-metric ablation establishes principled selection criteria (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Encoder-quality boundary with evidence gradient.} Against 384d MiniLM, the HS advantage is confirmatory at 7--8B (+20.1 to +21.9pp, HB corrected, nested CV validated), significant but position-competitive at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp), and exploratory at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, position-mediated). Against 1024d BGE-large, only OT-7B survives correction (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Negative results define method boundaries.} No alternative method consistently matches supervised probing at any evidence tier (\\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD/CUSUM fail on gradual transitions; Mahalanobis distance finds near-zero separation; RepE shows inconsistent performance across models (competitive on QwQ-32B, poor elsewhere); cross-model transfer fails under PCA alignment. Control task selectivity (11.1--16.8pp above chance; \\cref{app:control}) confirms genuine safety structure.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\noindent These findings provide initial characterization on one benchmark for hidden-state safety monitoring and identify layer selection as a critical confound that future work must address.\n",

    "oldString": "Precision, inverse FPR, and additive composite all derive from the same $2\\times2$ confusion matrix and therefore converge to the same layer by mathematical necessity; BA is threshold-free and provides independent confirmation.",
    "newString": "Precision, inverse FPR, and additive composite all derive from the same $2\\times2$ confusion matrix and empirically converge to the same layer, consistent with their shared structure but not mathematically guaranteed (each metric defines a distinct optimization landscape over layers); BA is threshold-free and provides independent confirmation.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Problem Setup and Methods}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Safety Commitment Point}\n\\label{sec:commitment-point}\n\nWhen a reasoning model is jailbroken, its chain-of-thought (CoT) transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation. We define the \\textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP) as the first reasoning step after which safety reasoning permanently ceases; traces with oscillating safety deliberation are resolved by taking the final irreversible transition (\\cref{sec:limitations}). Steps before CP are \\emph{pre-commitment} ($y_t = 0$); steps at or after CP are \\emph{post-commitment} ($y_t = 1$). The detection task produces a score $s_t \\in [0, 1]$ estimating whether the model has passed its CP by step $t$.\n\nWe study four reasoning models spanning two architectures and two scales (\\cref{tab:models}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Arch.} & \\textbf{Params} & \\textbf{Layers} & \\textbf{Hidden} & \\textbf{$n$ (total\\,/\\,test)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nR1-8B & Llama & 8B & 32 & 4096 & 209\\,/\\,43 \\\\\nOT-7B & Qwen & 7B & 28 & 3584 & 228\\,/\\,47 \\\\\nR1-32B & Qwen & 32B & 64 & 5120 & 100\\,/\\,20 \\\\\nQwQ-32B & Qwen & 32B & 64 & 5120 & 95\\,/\\,19 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Models studied. R1-8B and R1-32B are distilled from DeepSeek-R1~\\citep{deepseekr1}; QwQ-32B~\\citep{qwq} is a native reasoning model; OT-7B~\\citep{openthinker} is trained with STILL~\\citep{still} on Qwen2.5-7B. Data: HarmThoughts~\\citep{harmthoughts} with step-level CP annotations. Split: 60/20/20.}\n\\label{tab:models}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Probe Architecture}\n\\label{sec:probe-architecture}\n\n\\paragraph{Hidden-state (HS) probes.} At each step $t$, we extract the last-token hidden state $\\mathbf{h}_t^{(\\ell)} \\in \\mathbb{R}^d$ from layer $\\ell$ and train an L2-regularized logistic regression classifier (balanced class weights, $C{=}1.0$, LBFGS, max\\_iter${}=2000$) on binary CP labels. Sweeping $C \\in [0.01, 100]$ yields $<$2pp BA variation (\\cref{sec:experiments}), confirming robustness to this default. Layer selection is analyzed in \\cref{sec:layer-sensitivity}.\n\n\\paragraph{Text probes.} Each step's text is encoded with all-MiniLM-L6-v2~\\citep{wang2020minilm} ($\\mathbb{R}^{384}$), a distilled sentence encoder from the Sentence-BERT family~\\citep{reimers2019sentence}. The same logistic regression is applied as a surface-level modality baseline.\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal windowing.} For window size $W$, we concatenate features from steps $[t{-}W{+}1, \\ldots, t]$. We evaluate $W \\in \\{1, 3, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25\\}$ and select per-model on validation. GRU sequence models are also evaluated under two protocols: a 70/30 train/test split (primary) and the same 60/20/20 split used for LR experiments (for fair comparison; \\cref{tab:gru_multi_seeds,app:gru_details}).\n\n\\subsection{Layer Selection Sensitivity}\n\\label{sec:layer-sensitivity}\n\nLayer selection is a researcher degree of freedom that can reverse modality comparison conclusions. We evaluate five metrics: balanced accuracy (BA), precision, and inverse FPR are standard probing metrics~\\citep{belinkov2022probing}; crossing rate and additive composite come from the safety detection literature. A multiplicative composite (BA $\\times$ crossing rate) serves as the \\emph{crossing-rate composite} (CR-composite) baseline, so named because it incorporates crossing rate in the selection criterion. Metrics evaluating probe performance at a fixed decision threshold (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) form the \\emph{threshold-based} selection. This classification is an empirical observation from metric disagreement, not an a priori design choice; the ablation in \\cref{tab:layer_selection} is a reproducible empirical fact independent of our framing. Crossing rate inflates at shallow layers where representations are compressed (inter-step cosine distances 1.4--2.7$\\times$ smaller; \\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), so threshold crossings reflect noise rather than signal. This compression is an independently verifiable geometric property of shallow-layer representations, not an artifact of probe training. Precision, inverse FPR, and additive composite all derive from the same $2\\times2$ confusion matrix and therefore converge to the same layer by mathematical necessity; BA is threshold-free and provides independent confirmation. The ablation diagnoses which \\emph{aspect} drives layer selection bias. The effective number of independent tests is four (one per model), reflected in our HB correction. Results in \\cref{sec:layer-results}; full per-layer sweeps in \\cref{app:layer_sweep}.\n\nWe acknowledge a garden-of-forking-paths concern~\\citep{gelman2013garden}: the CR-composite vs.\\ threshold-based distinction was identified during analysis, not pre-registered, and the confirmatory analyses (random-CP decomposition, cosine volatility; \\cref{app:random_cp,app:cosine_volatility}) were developed post-hoc. However, the five-metric ablation (\\cref{tab:layer_selection}) is a reproducible empirical fact, and the mechanism (shallow-layer compression inflating crossing rate) is independently verifiable from representation geometry.\n\n\\subsection{Baseline Methods}\n\\label{sec:baselines}\n\nWe evaluate seven alternatives to contextualize probe performance (\\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD~\\citep{adams2007bayesian} and CUSUM~\\citep{page1954cusum} for change-point detection; Mahalanobis distance following TrajGuard~\\citep{trajguard}; RepE mean-difference directions~\\citep{zou2023representation}; two-layer MLP probes; CCS-adapted~\\citep{burns2023discovering}; and cross-model transfer via PCA. A position-only baseline ($t/T$) controls for temporal artifacts. Detailed configurations in \\cref{app:bocpd_config,app:transfer_details}.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}\n\\label{sec:evaluation}\n\nWe report balanced accuracy, precision, pre-CP FPR, coverage, and lead time. We exclude F1, AUROC, and MCC: F1 conflates precision and recall (our coverage metric already captures recall-like behavior at the trace level); AUROC is threshold-free and cannot diagnose the layer selection bias that manifests at fixed decision thresholds; MCC is redundant with BA under balanced class priors and adds no discriminative information for layer selection. Detection requires $K{=}5$ consecutive threshold crossings, selected on the R1-8B validation set and held fixed across all models as the value balancing coverage ($\\geq$70\\%) and safe-trace false positive rate ($\\leq$50\\%); \\cref{tab:k_ablation} confirms that the HS vs.\\ text conclusion direction is consistent at $K{=}3$, $5$, and $7$, with coverage and FPR trading off monotonically. Modality comparisons use BCa bootstrap (10{,}000 resamples, resampling traces as clusters: each trace is an independent unit and all its steps are included or excluded together, preserving within-trace dependence)~\\citep{efron1993bootstrap,diciccio1996bootstrap} with Holm-Bonferroni step-down (3 metrics $\\times$ 4 models $= 12$ tests, $\\alpha{=}0.05$); the 4-test framework yields identical conclusions (\\cref{app:holm_bonferroni}). For 32B models ($n \\leq 20$), the exact permutation test is the primary inference method; BCa confidence intervals are reported for comparability but may have reduced coverage at these sample sizes. Within each model, the three metrics are positively correlated, so the correction is stricter than under independence. Control task selectivity~\\citep{hewitt2019control} validates genuine structure (\\cref{app:control}). $\\dagger$ marks sample-limited test sets ($n \\leq 20$): R1-32B ($n{=}20$) and QwQ-32B ($n{=}19$).\n",

    "oldString": "\\item \\textbf{Layer selection sensitivity.} We identify a systematic bias in composite metrics that reverses HS probe conclusions at 32B scale. A five-metric ablation establishes principled selection criteria (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).",
    "newString": "\\item \\textbf{Metric-induced layer selection bias.} We identify a metric-induced systematic bias: crossing-rate composite metrics systematically select shallow layers, reversing HS probe conclusions at 32B scale. A five-metric ablation establishes principled selection criteria (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nReasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \\textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Before building production detectors, a prior question must be answered: can current probing methods reliably distinguish pre-CP from post-CP reasoning steps, and under what conditions? This paper provides a systematic characterization of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect.\n\nExisting safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. Before asking which modality is better, a prior methodological question must be answered: how sensitive are probe conclusions to the choice of layer selection metric? HS probes require choosing which transformer layer to monitor, a researcher degree of freedom whose metric sensitivity has received almost no scrutiny. We show this choice alone can reverse a study's central conclusion.\n\nWe present an initial characterization across four reasoning models---primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models), with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence---at two scales (7--8B, 32B) on the HarmThoughts benchmark. Our central finding is methodological: the choice of layer selection metric entirely determines whether an HS advantage is observed. A composite metric (balanced accuracy $\\times$ crossing rate) selects shallow layers for 32B models because shallow representations compress directional information, so a linear probe operates near the noise floor and threshold crossings reflect noise rather than semantic transitions. At these layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage.\n\nThreshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) share a common confusion matrix and empirically converge; BA independently confirms deep-layer selection (\\cref{tab:layer_selection}). At these layers, the HS advantage is graded: \\emph{confirmatory} at 7--8B (precision +20.1 to +21.9pp, nested CV validated; \\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}), \\emph{significant} but position-competitive at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp; \\cref{sec:32b-analysis}), and \\emph{exploratory} at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, position-mediated, below MDE; \\cref{sec:experiments}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Layer selection sensitivity.} We identify a systematic bias in composite metrics that reverses HS probe conclusions at 32B scale. A five-metric ablation establishes principled selection criteria (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Encoder-quality boundary with evidence gradient.} Against 384d MiniLM, the HS advantage is confirmatory at 7--8B (+20.1 to +21.9pp, HB corrected, nested CV validated), significant but position-competitive at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp), and exploratory at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, position-mediated). Against 1024d BGE-large, only OT-7B survives correction (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Negative results define method boundaries.} No alternative method consistently matches supervised probing at any evidence tier (\\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD/CUSUM fail on gradual transitions; Mahalanobis distance finds near-zero separation; RepE shows inconsistent performance across models (competitive on QwQ-32B, poor elsewhere); cross-model transfer fails under PCA alignment. Control task selectivity (11.1--16.8pp above chance; \\cref{app:control}) confirms genuine safety structure.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\noindent These findings provide initial characterization on one benchmark for hidden-state safety monitoring and identify layer selection as a critical confound that future work must address.\n",

    "originalFile": "Reasoning models with chain-of-thought (CoT) can self-jailbreak: their reasoning traces transition from safe deliberation to committed harmful generation without adversarial input. Detecting the commitment point (CP)---the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases---requires monitoring internal representations or generated text. We study four reasoning LLMs, primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models) with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence, on HarmThoughts and provide initial characterization of hidden-state (HS) vs.\\ text probe capabilities on this benchmark. The comparison depends critically on which transformer layer is monitored: a crossing-rate composite metric masks the HS advantage at 32B scale by selecting shallow layers where representation compression inflates threshold crossings. Threshold-based layer selection recovers HS precision gains of +19.2 to +21.9pp against 384d MiniLM (Holm-Bonferroni corrected); this advantage diminishes with stronger encoders (384d: 4/4 models pass HB $\\to$ 1024d BGE-large: 1/4). The 7--8B results are confirmatory (nested CV validated): precision and balanced accuracy confirmed; R1-8B FPR is significant but seed-sensitive (3/5 seeds pass HB). The 32B results are exploratory$^\\dagger$ ($^\\dagger$\\,sample-limited, $n \\leq 20$): QwQ-32B is significant but position-competitive; R1-32B is position-mediated and below minimum detectable effect. Layer selection sensitivity is the robust methodological finding; the empirical HS advantage is conditional on encoder quality, model scale, and position control.\n",

The three threshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) share a common confusion matrix and empirically converge to the same deep layer---L63 (98\\%) for both 32B models---consistent with their shared structure, though not mathematically guaranteed. Pure balanced accuracy, a threshold-free metric, selects layers that vary across data splits and models (7--98\\% depth under 60/20/20; \\cref{tab:ba_layer_comparison}), itself demonstrating that layer selection is a consequential methodological choice. The total exploration space spans 5 metrics $\\times$ 7 window sizes $\\times$ 28--64 layers (model-dependent); HB correction covers the final 12 model-metric tests, not this full search (\\cref{app:hyperparameter_robustness}). The mechanism is that shallow layers produce highly compressed representations with small inter-step margins: step-to-step cosine distances are 1.4--2.7$\\times$ smaller than at deep layers across all models (\\cref{app:cosine_volatility}). A linear probe in this compressed space operates near the noise floor, generating frequent threshold crossings that inflate crossing rate independently of discriminative power (\\cref{sec:layer-sensitivity}).",
          " ",
          " \\begin{table}[t]",
          " \\centering"
        ]
      }
    ],

_source line 588 · 2026-05-19T16:23:37.907Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_018oTE9U3Ajpz4EfsD4VEtKg`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom.}
4	Our five-metric ablation (\cref{sec:layer-results}) shows that crossing rate reverses the central finding via shallow-layer representation compression (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), confirmed as label-independent (\cref{app:random_cp}) and likely generalizable to any threshold-based detection study. This separates the two contributions: layer selection sensitivity (Contribution~1) follows from representation geometry regardless of sample size and is likely to generalize beyond HarmThoughts; the empirical HS advantage (Contribution~2) is sample- and dataset-dependent, and its magnitude may not transfer to other benchmarks or annotation schemes. The threshold-based layers are the deepest in the network (L63/64 for 32B), consistent with late-layer safety features~\citep{wollschlager2025geometry}; however, R1-32B's residualized selectivity is only 3.0pp (\cref{app:control}), below the 5pp threshold of \citet{hewitt2019control}.\looseness=-1
5	
6	\paragraph{Encoder-quality boundary.}
7	The HS advantage is significant against 384d MiniLM (confirmatory at 7--8B; exploratory at 32B) but attenuated against 1024d BGE-large (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (+16.8 to +23.1pp), providing partial evidence against a pure capacity explanation. The HS advantage represents information not yet captured by current sentence encoders rather than a fundamental modality difference~\citep{boxo2025linear_probes}; whether stronger future encoders~\citep{wang2024e5mistral} could close the gap entirely bounds the shelf life of these claims.\looseness=-1
8	
9	\paragraph{Guidance for future research.}
10	Converging evidence (oracle analysis, PCA capacity control, control task selectivity, position residualization) supports genuine safety representations, but causal validation via DAS~\citep{geiger2025causal} or ITI~\citep{li2024iti} is needed. Under standard linear position control, 3/4 models retain significant non-positional HS advantage; under stricter quadratic control, only the two 7--8B models remain significant (\cref{tab:position_quadratic}), consistent with the exploratory status of 32B results. Our work diagnoses the layer selection problem; \citet{pais2026linear_probe_scaling} propose a complementary solution via multi-layer ensembling that bypasses single-layer brittleness. We propose---but have not validated---a multi-metric audit protocol: compute $\geq$3 metrics (threshold-free and threshold-based), flag metric disagreement, and verify cosine volatility at candidate layers (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}). Raising $K$ yields low-FPR operating points ($K{=}10$: 8\% FPR at 21\% coverage; \cref{app:deployment_operating_points}). Broader validation across architectures, datasets, and attack types is needed before deployment.\looseness=-1
11	
```

</details>

    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\\label{sec:related-work}\n\n\\paragraph{Safety in Reasoning Models.}\nReasoning models introduce a qualitatively new failure mode: the model's own chain of thought can transition from safety-aware deliberation to harmful generation without adversarial input modification~\\cite{wang2025lrmsafety}.\n\n\\textit{Attack characterization.} Self-jailbreak has been documented with step-level annotations~\\cite{harmthoughts}, with longer chains increasing circumvention probability~\\cite{selfjailbreak}. H-CoT~\\cite{hcot2025} shows reasoning chains can be externally hijacked. Unfaithful reasoning~\\cite{chen2025unfaithful,turpin2023language} further motivates hidden-state monitoring that bypasses text. Safety-aligned models maintain refusal intentions during thinking but experience a sharp drop at output generation~\\cite{yin2025refusal_cliff}, a phenomenon our temporal probes target. Recent work further documents adversarial reasoning capabilities and information leakage in reasoning traces~\\cite{hagendorff2026lrm_jailbreak,hu2026refusal_dynamics,ahrend2026safer_traces}, providing additional motivation for hidden-state monitoring.\n\n\\textit{Detection.} CoT monitoring can detect reward hacking but optimization pressure promotes obfuscation~\\cite{baker2025monitoring}. CoT monitorability is valuable but fragile~\\cite{korbak2025cot_monitorability}. GuardReasoner~\\cite{xiang2025guardreasoner} trains a dedicated reasoning-based guard model.\n\n\\textit{Mitigation.} SafeThink~\\cite{safethink} steers early reasoning toward safety, SafeRemind~\\cite{saferemind} inserts entropy-triggered reminders, and Chain-of-Guardrails~\\cite{mao2025selfjailbreak_reasoning} proposes guardrail insertion during reasoning. None of these conducts a systematic modality comparison for commitment detection.\n\n\\paragraph{Hidden-State Safety Detection.}\nInternal activations can detect safety violations in standard LLMs. \\citet{azaria2023internal} demonstrate that internal states encode whether a model's output is truthful, motivating representation-based monitoring. \\citet{zhou2024alignment} show safety-relevant information distributes across layers, while \\citet{qi2025shallow_alignment} find that alignment operates primarily over early output tokens. \\citet{arditi2024refusal} identify a single refusal direction, but \\citet{zhao2025harmfulness} show harmfulness and refusal are encoded separately. \\citet{joad2026refusal_multi} reveal eleven category-specific refusal directions, and \\citet{wollschlager2025geometry} formalize multi-dimensionality through concept cones, consistent with our finding that single-direction methods are insufficient. TrajGuard~\\cite{trajguard} uses Mahalanobis distance from safe trajectories; SafeSwitch~\\cite{safeswitch} trains hidden-state classifiers; SIREN~\\cite{siren} monitors internal representations during generation; SafeDream~\\cite{safedream2026} proposes proactive world models for detection; HiddenDetect~\\cite{jiang2025hiddendetect} extends hidden-state monitoring to vision-language models; SafeInt~\\cite{safeint2025} applies representation intervention for defense; RCS~\\cite{rcs2026} uses Mahalanobis-based contrastive scoring for jailbreak detection. These methods assume threats from external input manipulation and operate at the token level on instruction-tuned models. The self-jailbreak setting differs because transitions are internal and span multiple reasoning steps. \\citet{kadali2025internal} decompose internal layer representations to separate jailbreak from benign prompts; their results confirm that layer-wise structure carries safety-relevant signal. We reproduce TrajGuard (\\cref{sec:negative}) and find its geometric approach fails because discriminative signal resides in minor PCA components.\n\n\\paragraph{Probing Methodology and Layer Selection.}\nCausal abstraction~\\cite{geiger2025causal} formalizes when high-level causal models faithfully describe neural computation, grounding use of internal representations. Linear probing spans layer-wise classifiers~\\cite{alain2017understanding}, sentence probing~\\cite{conneau2018probing}, and surveys~\\cite{belinkov2019analysis,belinkov2022probing,rogers2020primer}. Control tasks~\\cite{hewitt2019control} distinguish structure from memorization (we adopt these; \\cref{app:control}). Information-theoretic and causal formulations address probe complexity~\\cite{voita2020information,pimentel2020information,ravfogel2020null,ravfogel2022rlace,belrose2023leace,elazar2021amnesic}. \\citet{marks2024geometry} find emergent linear structure in LLM truth representations, consistent with our safety commitment separability. That information distributes non-uniformly across layers is well established~\\cite{tenney2019bert,jawahar2019bert}. Our contribution is orthogonal: the known layer-wise information gradient interacts with metric choice to produce systematic selection bias in safety probes. Most directly, \\citet{boxo2025linear_probes} show linear probes can rely on textual evidence rather than deeper representations; our metric bias analysis provides a complementary mechanism: shallow-layer probes exploit compressed representations that inflate crossing rate, a geometric effect consistent with text-leakage at layers where representations have not yet diverged from surface features. \\citet{alvarez2026transport_geometry} apply hidden-state geometry to step-level hallucination detection concurrently with our work. Despite this foundation, layer selection remains underexamined: most studies select by a single metric without sensitivity analysis, a practice we show can reverse conclusions (\\cref{sec:layer-results}). \\citet{pais2026linear_probe_scaling} show that multi-layer ensembling recovers performance where single-layer probes fail, a complementary solution to the sensitivity problem we diagnose. Our approach is correlational; causal validation~\\citep{geiger2025causal,belrose2023leace,li2024iti} is needed to confirm probed directions are causally active.\n\n\\paragraph{Alternative Detection Approaches.}\nBOCPD~\\cite{adams2007bayesian} detects abrupt distributional shifts; CCS~\\cite{burns2023discovering} discovers latent truth directions via consistency constraints; RepE~\\cite{zou2023representation} identifies concept directions through mean differences. We adapt all three to temporal commitment detection (\\cref{sec:negative}). BOCPD and CUSUM fail because commitment is gradual. CCS provides no gain because its consistency objective misaligns with temporal structure. RepE shows inconsistent performance across models (0.756 on QwQ, 0.559 on R1-32B). These failures motivate supervised probes with principled layer selection as the minimal effective methodology.\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_019nt84iAXtyBW2RYzkc9Yui`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom.}
4	Our five-metric ablation (\cref{sec:layer-results}) shows that crossing rate reverses the central finding via shallow-layer representation compression (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), confirmed as label-independent (\cref{app:random_cp}) and likely generalizable to any threshold-based detection study. This separates the two contributions: layer selection sensitivity (Contribution~1) follows from representation geometry regardless of sample size and is likely to generalize beyond HarmThoughts; the empirical HS advantage (Contribution~2) is sample- and dataset-dependent, and its magnitude may not transfer to other benchmarks or annotation schemes. The threshold-based layers are the deepest in the network (L63/64 for 32B), consistent with late-layer safety features~\citep{wollschlager2025geometry}; however, R1-32B's residualized selectivity is only 3.0pp (\cref{app:control}), below the 5pp threshold of \citet{hewitt2019control}.\looseness=-1
5	
6	\paragraph{Encoder-quality boundary.}
7	The HS advantage is significant against 384d MiniLM (confirmatory at 7--8B; exploratory at 32B) but attenuated against 1024d BGE-large (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (+16.8 to +23.1pp), providing partial evidence against a pure capacity explanation. The HS advantage represents information not yet captured by current sentence encoders rather than a fundamental modality difference~\citep{boxo2025linear_probes}; whether stronger future encoders~\citep{wang2024e5mistral} could close the gap entirely bounds the shelf life of these claims.\looseness=-1
8	
9	\paragraph{Guidance for future research.}
10	Converging evidence (oracle analysis, PCA capacity control, control task selectivity, position residualization) supports genuine safety representations, but causal validation via DAS~\citep{geiger2025causal} or ITI~\citep{li2024iti} is needed. Under linear residualization, 3/4 models retain significant non-positional HS advantage; under quadratic control (a sensitivity analysis, not the primary specification), only 2/4 remain significant---QwQ-32B's advantage is absorbed by the $(t/T)^2$ term (\cref{tab:position_quadratic}). The difference indicates the 32B HS advantage may partly reflect non-linear position artifacts, consistent with the exploratory status of those results. Our work diagnoses the layer selection problem; \citet{pais2026linear_probe_scaling} propose a complementary solution via multi-layer ensembling that bypasses single-layer brittleness. We propose---but have not validated---a multi-metric audit protocol: compute $\geq$3 metrics (threshold-free and threshold-based), flag metric disagreement, and verify cosine volatility at candidate layers (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}). Raising $K$ yields low-FPR operating points ($K{=}10$: 8\% FPR at 21\% coverage; \cref{app:deployment_operating_points}). Broader validation across architectures, datasets, and attack types is needed before deployment.\looseness=-1
11	
```

  "toolUseResult": {
    "type": "text",
    "file": {
      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/docs/paper/sections/discussion.tex",
      "content": "\\section{Discussion}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom.}\nOur five-metric ablation (\\cref{sec:layer-results}) shows that crossing rate reverses the central finding via shallow-layer representation compression (\\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), confirmed as label-independent (\\cref{app:random_cp}) and likely generalizable to any threshold-based detection study. This separates the two contributions: layer selection sensitivity (Contribution~1) follows from representation geometry regardless of sample size and is likely to generalize beyond HarmThoughts; the empirical HS advantage (Contribution~2) is sample- and dataset-dependent, and its magnitude may not transfer to other benchmarks or annotation schemes. The threshold-based layers are the deepest in the network (L63/64 for 32B), consistent with late-layer safety features~\\citep{wollschlager2025geometry}; however, R1-32B's residualized selectivity is only 3.0pp (\\cref{app:control}), below the 5pp threshold of \\citet{hewitt2019control}.\\looseness=-1\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder-quality boundary.}\nThe HS advantage is significant against 384d MiniLM (confirmatory at 7--8B; exploratory at 32B) but attenuated against 1024d BGE-large (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (+16.8 to +23.1pp), providing partial evidence against a pure capacity explanation. The HS advantage represents information not yet captured by current sentence encoders rather than a fundamental modality difference~\\citep{boxo2025linear_probes}; whether stronger future encoders~\\citep{wang2024e5mistral} could close the gap entirely bounds the shelf life of these claims.\\looseness=-1\n\n\\paragraph{Guidance for future research.}\nConverging evidence (oracle analysis, PCA capacity control, control task selectivity, position residualization) supports genuine safety representations, but causal validation via DAS~\\citep{geiger2025causal} or ITI~\\citep{li2024iti} is needed. Under linear residualization, 3/4 models retain significant non-positional HS advantage; under quadratic control (a sensitivity analysis, not the primary specification), only 2/4 remain significant---QwQ-32B's advantage is absorbed by the $(t/T)^2$ term (\\cref{tab:position_quadratic}). The difference indicates the 32B HS advantage may partly reflect non-linear position artifacts, consistent with the exploratory status of those results. Our work diagnoses the layer selection problem; \\citet{pais2026linear_probe_scaling} propose a complementary solution via multi-layer ensembling that bypasses single-layer brittleness. We propose---but have not validated---a multi-metric audit protocol: compute $\\geq$3 metrics (threshold-free and threshold-based), flag metric disagreement, and verify cosine volatility at candidate layers (\\cref{app:cosine_volatility}). Raising $K$ yields low-FPR operating points ($K{=}10$: 8\\% FPR at 21\\% coverage; \\cref{app:deployment_operating_points}). Broader validation across architectures, datasets, and attack types is needed before deployment.\\looseness=-1\n",
      "numLines": 11,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 11
    }
  },
  "sourceToolAssistantUUID": "2484cee6-a3a6-4de1-8e02-50f4aa7b7120",

1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	Layer selection is a critical researcher degree of freedom that can reverse a study's central conclusion; the multi-metric audit protocol (\cref{sec:discussion}) would have prevented our initial spurious finding. The 7--8B HS advantage is confirmatory (nested CV validated); the 32B results$^\dagger$ are exploratory and the advantage narrows as text encoders improve (1/4 HB at 1024d). Contribution~1 (layer selection sensitivity) generalizes from representation geometry; Contribution~2 (HS advantage magnitude) is initial characterization on one benchmark requiring further validation. Causal validation and LLM-based encoders~\citep{wang2024e5mistral,li2024iti} are immediate next steps.\looseness=-1
4

        "lines": [
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Position mediation.} Position ($t/T$) accounts for 9--20pp of balanced accuracy,\\footnote{R1-32B's 20.4pp drop reflects $W{=}1$; at $W{\\geq}3$, drops are 9--17pp.} but all models retain non-positional signal above chance (all $p{<}0.001$; \\cref{app:position_residual}). Three of four models retain significant non-positional HS advantage (residualized gap +3.7 to +6.9pp, HB corrected); R1-32B's gap is +1.9pp ($p{=}0.914$, $n{=}20$). Adding a quadratic position term $(t/T)^2$ does not change significance conclusions for the 7--8B models. However, QwQ-32B's advantage is fully absorbed by the quadratic term (\\cref{tab:position_quadratic}), suggesting its residualized advantage contains a non-linear position component. Temporal windowing reduces position dependence (24.3pp drop at $W{=}3$ vs.\\ 16.5pp at $W{=}15$).\\looseness=-1",
          " ",
          "-\\paragraph{Caveats.} Nested 5-fold cross-validation confirms the HS precision advantage for both 7--8B models (10/10 folds; \\cref{app:nested_cv}), with shrinkage of 13--25\\% relative to holdout estimates, ruling out hyperparameter overfitting. OT-7B nested CV selects L21--27 vs.\\ holdout L16 vs.\\ CR-composite L0; the +21.9pp holdout estimate is an upper bound, and the nested CV shrunk estimate is the conservative reference. This inconsistency itself supports Contribution~1 (layer selection sensitivity). R1-32B achieves significance only at $W{=}1$ with 25\\% coverage; at $W{\\geq}3$, dimensionality prevents significance. Regularization $C \\in [0.01, 100]$ yields ${<}2$pp BA variation; decision thresholds are similarly stable (\\cref{app:threshold_sensitivity}). QwQ-32B HS lead time is negative ($-$1.2 to +1.9 steps): higher precision at later detection. False alarm rates remain 11--50\\%, so current methods characterize the phenomenon rather than constitute production detectors. Per-model details in \\cref{sec:32b-analysis}.",
          "+\\paragraph{Caveats.} Nested 5-fold cross-validation confirms the HS precision advantage for both 7--8B models (10/10 folds; \\cref{app:nested_cv}), with shrinkage of 13--25\\% relative to holdout estimates, ruling out hyperparameter overfitting. OT-7B nested CV selects L21--27 vs.\\ holdout L16 vs.\\ CR-composite L0; the +21.9pp holdout estimate is an upper bound, and the nested CV shrunk estimate is the conservative reference. Regularization $C \\in [0.01, 100]$ yields ${<}2$pp BA variation; decision thresholds are similarly stable (\\cref{app:threshold_sensitivity}). Based on the search results, here is the information about the HiddenDetect paper (arxiv 2502.14744):

The authors of this paper are: Yilei Jiang, Xinyan Gao, Tianshuo Peng, Yingshui Tan, Xiaoyong Zhu, Bo Zheng, and Xiangyu Yue.

The paper was authored by researchers from MMLab at The Chinese University of Hong Kong and Future Lab at Alibaba Group.

The paper was published on February 20, 2025, and introduces HiddenDetect, a novel tuning-free framework that harnesses internal model activations to enhance safety, with experimental results showing it surpasses state-of-the-art methods in detecting jailbreak attacks against LVLMs.

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Ksva5TKwTUPQqJVcjH3w58`</summary>

```text
49	\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}
50	\label{sec:evaluation}
51	
52	We report balanced accuracy, precision, pre-CP FPR, coverage, and lead time. We exclude F1, AUROC, and MCC: F1 conflates precision and recall (our coverage metric already captures recall-like behavior at the trace level); AUROC is threshold-free and cannot diagnose the layer selection bias that manifests at fixed decision thresholds; MCC is redundant with BA under balanced class priors and adds no discriminative information for layer selection. Detection requires $K{=}5$ consecutive threshold crossings, selected on the R1-8B validation set and held fixed across all models as the value balancing coverage ($\geq$70\%) and safe-trace false positive rate ($\leq$50\%); \cref{tab:k_ablation} confirms that the HS vs.\ text conclusion direction is consistent at $K{=}3$, $5$, and $7$, with coverage and FPR trading off monotonically. Modality comparisons use BCa bootstrap (10{,}000 resamples, resampling traces as clusters: each trace is an independent unit and all its steps are included or excluded together, preserving within-trace dependence)~\citep{efron1993bootstrap,diciccio1996bootstrap} with Holm-Bonferroni step-down (3 metrics $\times$ 4 models $= 12$ tests, $\alpha{=}0.05$); the 4-test framework yields identical conclusions (\cref{app:holm_bonferroni}). For 32B models ($n \leq 20$), the exact permutation test is the primary inference method; BCa confidence intervals are reported for comparability but may have reduced coverage at these sample sizes. Within each model, the three metrics are positively correlated, so the correction is stricter than under independence. Control task selectivity~\citep{hewitt2019control} validates genuine structure (\cref{app:control}). $\dagger$ marks sample-limited test sets ($n \leq 20$): R1-32B ($n{=}20$) and QwQ-32B ($n{=}19$).
53	
```

</details>

    "originalFile": "\\section{Problem Setup and Methods}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Safety Commitment Point}\n\\label{sec:commitment-point}\n\nWhen a reasoning model is jailbroken, its chain-of-thought (CoT) transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation. We define the \\textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP) as the first reasoning step after which safety reasoning permanently ceases; traces with oscillating safety deliberation are resolved by taking the final irreversible transition (\\cref{sec:limitations}). Steps before CP are \\emph{pre-commitment} ($y_t = 0$); steps at or after CP are \\emph{post-commitment} ($y_t = 1$). The detection task produces a score $s_t \\in [0, 1]$ estimating whether the model has passed its CP by step $t$.\n\nWe study four reasoning models spanning two architectures and two scales (\\cref{tab:models}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Arch.} & \\textbf{Params} & \\textbf{Layers} & \\textbf{Hidden} & \\textbf{$n$ (total\\,/\\,test)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nR1-8B & Llama & 8B & 32 & 4096 & 209\\,/\\,43 \\\\\nOT-7B & Qwen & 7B & 28 & 3584 & 228\\,/\\,47 \\\\\nR1-32B & Qwen & 32B & 64 & 5120 & 100\\,/\\,20 \\\\\nQwQ-32B & Qwen & 32B & 64 & 5120 & 95\\,/\\,19 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Models studied. R1-8B and R1-32B are distilled from DeepSeek-R1~\\citep{deepseekr1}; QwQ-32B~\\citep{qwq} is a native reasoning model; OT-7B~\\citep{openthinker} is trained with STILL~\\citep{still} on Qwen2.5-7B. Data: HarmThoughts~\\citep{harmthoughts} with step-level CP annotations. Split: 60/20/20.}\n\\label{tab:models}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Probe Architecture}\n\\label{sec:probe-architecture}\n\n\\paragraph{Hidden-state (HS) probes.} At each step $t$, we extract the last-token hidden state $\\mathbf{h}_t^{(\\ell)} \\in \\mathbb{R}^d$ from layer $\\ell$ and train an L2-regularized logistic regression classifier (balanced class weights, $C{=}1.0$, LBFGS, max\\_iter${}=2000$) on binary CP labels. Sweeping $C \\in [0.01, 100]$ yields $<$2pp BA variation (\\cref{sec:experiments}), confirming robustness to this default. Layer selection is analyzed in \\cref{sec:layer-sensitivity}.\n\n\\paragraph{Text probes.} Each step's text is encoded with all-MiniLM-L6-v2~\\citep{wang2020minilm} ($\\mathbb{R}^{384}$), a distilled sentence encoder from the Sentence-BERT family~\\citep{reimers2019sentence}. The same logistic regression is applied as a surface-level modality baseline.\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal windowing.} For window size $W$, we concatenate features from steps $[t{-}W{+}1, \\ldots, t]$. We evaluate $W \\in \\{1, 3, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25\\}$ and select per-model on validation. GRU sequence models are also evaluated under two protocols: a 70/30 train/test split (primary) and the same 60/20/20 split used for LR experiments (for fair comparison; \\cref{tab:gru_multi_seeds,app:gru_details}).\n\n\\subsection{Layer Selection Sensitivity}\n\\label{sec:layer-sensitivity}\n\nLayer selection is a researcher degree of freedom that can reverse modality comparison conclusions. We evaluate five metrics: balanced accuracy (BA), precision, and inverse FPR are standard probing metrics~\\citep{belinkov2022probing}; crossing rate and additive composite come from the safety detection literature. A multiplicative composite (BA $\\times$ crossing rate) serves as the \\emph{crossing-rate composite} (CR-composite) baseline, so named because it incorporates crossing rate in the selection criterion. Metrics evaluating probe performance at a fixed decision threshold (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) form the \\emph{threshold-based} selection. This classification is an empirical observation from metric disagreement, not an a priori design choice; the ablation in \\cref{tab:layer_selection} is a reproducible empirical fact independent of our framing. Crossing rate inflates at shallow layers where representations are compressed (inter-step cosine distances 1.4--2.7$\\times$ smaller; \\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), so threshold crossings reflect noise rather than signal. This compression is an independently verifiable geometric property of shallow-layer representations, not an artifact of probe training. Precision, inverse FPR, and additive composite all derive from the same $2\\times2$ confusion matrix and empirically converge to the same layer, consistent with their shared structure but not mathematically guaranteed (each metric defines a distinct optimization landscape over layers); BA is threshold-free and provides independent confirmation. The ablation diagnoses which \\emph{aspect} drives layer selection bias. The effective number of independent tests is four (one per model), reflected in our HB correction. Results in \\cref{sec:layer-results}; full per-layer sweeps in \\cref{app:layer_sweep}.\n\nWe acknowledge a garden-of-forking-paths concern~\\citep{gelman2013garden}: the CR-composite vs.\\ threshold-based distinction was identified during analysis, not pre-registered, and the confirmatory analyses (random-CP decomposition, cosine volatility; \\cref{app:random_cp,app:cosine_volatility}) were developed post-hoc. However, the five-metric ablation (\\cref{tab:layer_selection}) is a reproducible empirical fact, and the mechanism (shallow-layer compression inflating crossing rate) is independently verifiable from representation geometry.\n\n\\subsection{Baseline Methods}\n\\label{sec:baselines}\n\nWe evaluate seven alternatives to contextualize probe performance (\\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD~\\citep{adams2007bayesian} and CUSUM~\\citep{page1954cusum} for change-point detection; Mahalanobis distance following TrajGuard~\\citep{trajguard}; RepE mean-difference directions~\\citep{zou2023representation}; two-layer MLP probes; CCS-adapted~\\citep{burns2023discovering}; and cross-model transfer via PCA. A position-only baseline ($t/T$) controls for temporal artifacts. Detailed configurations in \\cref{app:bocpd_config,app:transfer_details}.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}\n\\label{sec:evaluation}\n\nWe report balanced accuracy, precision, pre-CP FPR, coverage, and lead time. We exclude F1, AUROC, and MCC: F1 conflates precision and recall (our coverage metric already captures recall-like behavior at the trace level); AUROC is threshold-free and cannot diagnose the layer selection bias that manifests at fixed decision thresholds; MCC is redundant with BA under balanced class priors and adds no discriminative information for layer selection. Detection requires $K{=}5$ consecutive threshold crossings, selected on the R1-8B validation set and held fixed across all models as the value balancing coverage ($\\geq$70\\%) and safe-trace false positive rate ($\\leq$50\\%); \\cref{tab:k_ablation} confirms that the HS vs.\\ text conclusion direction is consistent at $K{=}3$, $5$, and $7$, with coverage and FPR trading off monotonically. Modality comparisons use BCa bootstrap (10{,}000 resamples, resampling traces as clusters: each trace is an independent unit and all its steps are included or excluded together, preserving within-trace dependence)~\\citep{efron1993bootstrap,diciccio1996bootstrap} with Holm-Bonferroni step-down (3 metrics $\\times$ 4 models $= 12$ tests, $\\alpha{=}0.05$); the 4-test framework yields identical conclusions (\\cref{app:holm_bonferroni}). For 32B models ($n \\leq 20$), the exact permutation test is the primary inference method; BCa confidence intervals are reported for comparability but may have reduced coverage at these sample sizes. Within each model, the three metrics are positively correlated, so the correction is stricter than under independence. Control task selectivity~\\citep{hewitt2019control} validates genuine structure (\\cref{app:control}). $\\dagger$ marks sample-limited test sets ($n \\leq 20$): R1-32B ($n{=}20$) and QwQ-32B ($n{=}19$).\n",
